\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s}/N_{s+1}=0
such that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
.
Let
\mathbf{H}_{G,A}=(H_{G,A,s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
1\prec\mathbf{H}_{G,A}\prec\mathbf{N}
be a Gowers scale for
A
as guaranteed by
8.5
(ii)
and
\mathbf{H}_{G,B}=(H_{G,B,s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
1\prec\mathbf{H}_{G,B}\prec\mathbf{N}
a Gowers scale for
B
.
Let
\mathbf{H}=(H_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
U^{2}
good scale for
A
and
B
with
\mathbf{H}_{G,A},\mathbf{H}_{G,B}\prec\mathbf{H}\prec\mathbf{N}
.
Then, after passing to a subsequence of
(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{H})
, there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
and
b_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
such that one of the following holds:

(1’)

There exists
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
and sequences
k_{s},C_{s}\to\infty
and
\varepsilon_{s}\in(-1/2,1/2]
with
\varepsilon_{s}\to 0
and
\min_{q\in[k_{s}]}\|q(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geqslant\frac{C_{s}}{H_{s}}
such that for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
and all but
{\rm o}(N_{s})
many
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
, there exists a decomposition

(A-n)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}=(A_{n}\cup E_{n})-a_{n}

and

B\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}=(B_{s,0}\cup B_{s,1}\cup F_{s})-b_{0}

such that
A_{n},B_{s,0}\subseteq H
,
a_{n}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
,
B_{s,1}\subseteq\mathbb{N}\backslash H
with
|B_{s,1}|=\left(1-\frac{1}{h}-{\rm o}(1)\right)H_{s}
,
|E_{n}|={\rm o}(H_{s})
,
|F_{s}|={\rm o}(H_{s})
, and there are closed intervals
I_{n},J_{s}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that if
\phi_{s}:h\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{T}
is the map
\phi_{s}(m)=m(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})
for
m\in H
, then

A_{n}\subseteq\phi_{s}^{-1}(I_{n}),\hskip 10.00002ptB_{s,0}\subseteq\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s}),

and

|\phi_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\backslash A_{n}|,|\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\backslash B_{s,0}|={\rm o}(H_{s}).

Moreover,
\alpha=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{h}
and
\beta=1-\frac{1}{h}+\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}
for some
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
with
\alpha_{0}+\beta_{0}<1
, and
|I_{n}|=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
and
|J_{s}|=\beta_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
.

(2’)

There exist
a_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
such that
A\subseteq h\mathbb{N}-a_{0}
,
A+h\sim_{\mathbf{N}}A
, and
B\sim_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})-b_{0}
.

Proof.

We apply
10.1
.
For every
s\in\mathbb{N}
, either
(1)
or
(2)
from
10.1
holds.
We may pass to a subsequence such that
b_{s}
is a constant
b_{0}
and either
(1)
holds for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
or
(2)
holds for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose
(2)
holds for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then by
11.1
, we get the upgraded conclusion
(2’)
.

Suppose
(1)
holds for every
s\in\mathbb{N}
.
We may assume that
(2)
does not hold.
That is,
\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{|B_{s,0}|}{H_{s}}\neq 0
.
Therefore,
\beta_{0}=1-h(1-\beta)>0
.
We now pass to a further subsequence so that the limit
\theta=\lim_{s\to\infty}\theta_{s}
exists and put
\varepsilon_{s}=\theta_{s}-\theta
.
The property
(1’)
, save the final sentence beginning with “moreover,” then follows from
(1)
.
For the “moreover” statement, we have already shown
\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
.
Letting
\alpha_{0}=h\alpha
, we have
\alpha_{0}>0
and, since
\alpha+\beta<1
,

\alpha_{0}+\beta_{0}=h\alpha+1-h(1-\beta)=1-h(1-\alpha-\beta)<1.

It remains only to show that we may take the intervals
I_{n}
and
J_{s}
to satisfy
|I_{n}|=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
and
|J_{s}|=\beta_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
.
We will show
|I_{n}|=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
.
The condition
|J_{s}|=\beta_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
follows by a similar argument.
Since
\mathbf{H}\succ\mathbf{H}_{G,A}
, we have that
|\left((A-n)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\right)|=\left(\alpha+{\rm o}(1)\right)H_{s}
for all but
{\rm o}(N_{s})
many
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
, so

\left|\phi_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\right|=(\alpha+{\rm o}(1))H_{s}.

Letting
\tilde{\phi}_{s}(m)=\phi_{s}(hm)=hm\theta_{s}
, we thus have

\left|\tilde{\phi}_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor(H_{s}-1)/h\rfloor\}\right|=(\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1))\frac{H_{s}}{h}.

Note that
\tilde{\phi}_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})=\mathrm{Bohr}(h\theta_{s},I_{n})
, so we may assume
|I_{n}|=\delta_{n}
for
\delta_{n}=d(\mathrm{Bohr}(h\theta_{s},I_{n}))
.
Let
k^{\prime}_{s}=\min\left\{k_{s},\lfloor e^{\sqrt{C_{s}}}\rfloor\right\}
so that
\lim_{s\to\infty}k^{\prime}_{s}=\infty
and
\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\log k^{\prime}_{s}}{C_{s}}=0
.
Applying
5.3
with
Q=k^{\prime}_{s}/h
, we have

\displaystyle|\alpha_{0}-\delta_{n}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\left|\tilde{\phi}_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor(H_{s}-1)/h\rfloor\}\right|}{\lfloor(H_{s}-1)/h\rfloor+1}-\delta_{n}\right|+{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{h}{k^{\prime}_{s}}+\frac{\log(k^{\prime}_{s}/h)}{\left(\lfloor(H_{s}-1)/h\rfloor+1\right)C_{s}/H_{s}}+{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{k^{\prime}_{s}}+h\frac{\log(k^{\prime}_{s})}{C_{s}}+{\rm o}(1)={\rm o}(1),

so
|I_{n}|=\delta_{n}=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
as desired.
∎

12.  Eliminating rational frequencies

In this section, we refine the conclusion of
11.4
, showing that in case
(1’)
, the frequency
\theta
must be irrational.

Proposition 12.1
.

Let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
d(A)=\alpha>0
and
d(B)=\beta>0
such that
\alpha+\beta<1
.
Suppose
B
meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence with
\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s}/N_{s+1}=0
such that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
.
Let
\mathbf{H}_{G,A}=(H_{G,A,s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
1\prec\mathbf{H}_{G,A}\prec\mathbf{N}
be a Gowers scale for
A
as guaranteed by
8.5
(ii)
and
\mathbf{H}_{G,B}=(H_{G,B,s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
1\prec\mathbf{H}_{G,B}\prec\mathbf{N}
a Gowers scale for
B
.
Let
\mathbf{H}=(H_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be a
U^{2}
good scale for
A
and
B
with
\mathbf{H}_{G,A},\mathbf{H}_{G,B}\prec\mathbf{H}\prec\mathbf{N}
.
If
A
and
B
satisfy
(1’)
from
11.4
, then
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

We prove
12.1
in several steps.
Let us fix the notation for the proof.
In addition to the notation in the statement of
12.1
, we adopt the notation from
11.4
(1’)
.
In summary, we have the following:

•

sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
d(A)=\alpha>0
,
d(B)=\beta>0
and
\alpha+\beta<1
, and
B
meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
,

•

an increasing sequence
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s}/N_{s+1}=0
such that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
,

•

Gowers scales
\mathbf{H}_{G,A}
and
\mathbf{H}_{G,B}
for
A
and
B
respectively as given by
8.5
(ii)
,

•

a
U^{2}
good scale
\mathbf{H}
for
A
and
B
with
\mathbf{H}_{G,A},\mathbf{H}_{G,B}\prec\mathbf{H}\prec\mathbf{N}
,

•

a natural number
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h<\frac{1}{1-\beta}<\frac{1}{\alpha}
,

•

an element
b_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}

•

positive real numbers
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
satisfying
\alpha_{0}=h\alpha
,
\beta_{0}=1-h(1-\beta)
, and
\alpha_{0}+\beta_{0}<1
,

•

an element
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
,

•

sequences
(k_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}},(C_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\lim_{s\to\infty}k_{s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}C_{s}=\infty
,

•

a sequence
\varepsilon_{s}\in(-1/2,1/2]
with
\varepsilon_{s}\to 0
such that
\min_{q\in[k_{s}]}\|q(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geqslant\frac{C_{s}}{H_{s}}
,

•

for
s\in\mathbb{N}
, a decomposition
B\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}=(B_{s,0}\cup B_{s,1}\cup F_{s})-b_{0}
such that
|F_{s}|={\rm o}(H_{s})
,
B_{s,1}\subseteq\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N}
with
|B_{s,1}|=\left(1-\frac{1}{h}-{\rm o}(1)\right)H_{s}
, and taking
\phi_{s}(m)=m(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})
, there exists a closed interval
J_{s}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
of length
|J_{s}|=\beta_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
such that
B_{s,0}\subseteq\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})
and

\left|\left(\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})\cap h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\right)\backslash B_{s,0}\right|={\rm o}(H_{s}),

and

•

for
s\in\mathbb{N}
and all but
{\rm o}(N_{s})
many
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
, an element
a_{n}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
and an interval
I_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
of length
|I_{n}|=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)
such that

\left|\left((A-n+a_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\right)\triangle\left(\phi_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}\right)\right|={\rm o}(H_{s}).

Note that for an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{T}
, we may write
\phi_{s}^{-1}(I)\cap h\mathbb{Z}=h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I)
, which will be useful for applying estimates from
Section

5
.
We will write

A_{n}=\left(h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})-a_{n}\right)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}

for the set approximating
(A-n)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}
.

Let us suppose for contradiction that
\theta
is rational.
Write
h\theta
in reduced terms as
h\theta=\frac{p}{q}
.
Observe that
\|q\varepsilon_{s}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|q(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geqslant\frac{C_{s}}{H_{s}}
, so
|\varepsilon_{s}|=\|\varepsilon_{s}\|_{\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Q}}\geqslant\frac{C_{s}}{qH_{s}}
.
In particular,
\lim_{s\to\infty}|\varepsilon_{s}|H_{s}=\infty
.

Claim 1.
\alpha_{0}<\frac{1}{q}
.

We prove the claim by contradiction, considering the two cases
\alpha_{0}>\frac{1}{q}
and
\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{q}
separately.

Suppose
\alpha_{0}>\frac{1}{q}
.
Choose
T\in\mathbb{N}
such that
B\cap[T]
meets every residue class mod
qh
.
By Corollary
5.6
, for all large
s
(large enough so that
(q+2T)h|\varepsilon_{s}|<\alpha_{0}
), the set
\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})
contains a full residue class mod
q
in every interval of length at most
2T
.
Therefore,
A_{n}=h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})-a_{n}
contains a full residue class mod
qh
in every interval of length at most
2hT
.
Therefore,

\left(A_{n}\cap\{x,x+1,\ldots,x+2T\}\right)+(B\cap[T])\supseteq\{x+T,x+T+1,\ldots,x+2T\}

for every
x\in[H_{s}-2T]
, so
A_{n}+(B\cap[T])\supseteq\{T,T+1,\ldots,H_{s}\}
.
It follows that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+(B\cap[T]))=1
, which contradicts the assumption
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta<1
.

If
\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{q}
, a similar argument applies but with a small modification.
Since
|I_{n}|=\frac{1}{q}+{\rm o}(1)
, the set
\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})
can be partitioned into intervals of length on the order of
|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}
corresponding to levels sets of
m\mapsto\lfloor mq\varepsilon_{s}-\min(I_{n})\rfloor
such that on each interval,
\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})
is equal to a single residue class mod
q
(up to boundedly many elements).
Adding
B\cap[T]
to
A_{n}
therefore produces a set with density
1-{\rm O}(T|\varepsilon_{s}|)
in every interval of length at least
|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}
, so we again conclude
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+(B\cap[T]))=1
, which is a contradiction.

This proves Claim 1.

Claim 2.
\beta_{0}\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{q}
.

Let
K_{s}=\min\left\{\lfloor|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1/2}\rfloor,\lfloor C_{s}^{1/2}\rfloor\right\}
.
Then

\lim_{s\to\infty}K_{s}=\infty\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lim_{s\to\infty}|\varepsilon_{s}|K_{s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{K_{s}}{|\varepsilon_{s}|H_{s}}=0.

Put
M_{s}=\lfloor(|\varepsilon_{s}|K_{s})^{-1}\rfloor
and
L_{s}=4h\lceil|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}K_{s}\rceil
.
Subdivide
[H_{s}/h]=J_{s,1}\cup\ldots\cup J_{s,\ell_{s}}
into intervals of lengths between
\frac{L_{s}}{4h}
and
\frac{L_{s}}{2h}
.
By construction,

K_{s}M_{s}\leqslant|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}\leqslant K_{s}^{-1}\frac{L_{s}}{4h}\leqslant K_{s}^{-1}|J_{s,i}|

and

q^{2}|\varepsilon_{s}|+\frac{q}{K_{s}}={\rm o}(1),

so by Corollary
5.6
, for a
q\alpha_{0}-{\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(1)
proportion of points
x\in E_{n,i}\subseteq J_{s,i}
, the set
\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+M_{s}\}
consists of a single residue class mod
q
for each
i\in[\ell_{s}]
.
Let
E_{n}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\ell_{s}}E_{n,i}\subseteq[H_{s}/h]
.
Then
|E_{n}|=(q\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1))H_{s}
and
A_{n}
consists of a single residue class mod
qh
in each of the intervals
\{y+1,\ldots,y+hM_{s}\}
for
y\in E^{\prime}_{n}=\{hx-a_{n}+r:x\in E_{n},r\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}\}
.
Let
A^{\prime}_{n}=\bigcup_{y\in E^{\prime}_{n}}(A_{n}\cap\{y+1,\ldots,y+hM_{s}\})
.
Note that

\displaystyle\frac{|A^{\prime}_{n}|}{H_{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H_{s}}\frac{|A^{\prime}_{n}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+hM_{s}\}|}{hM_{s}}+{\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(1)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{y\in E^{\prime}_{n}}\frac{1}{qh}+{\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(1)

\displaystyle=\alpha+{\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(1).

We conclude that
|A_{n}\backslash A^{\prime}_{n}|={\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(H_{s})
.

Now let
T\in\mathbb{N}
such that
B\cap[T]
meets every residue class mod
qh
.
Then

\left|\big((n+A^{\prime}_{n})+(B\cap[T])\big)\backslash\big(\left(A+B\right)\cap\{n+1,\ldots,n+H_{s}\}\big)\right|={\rm o}(H_{s}),

and

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{n}+(B\cap[T])

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{y\in E^{\prime}_{n}}\left((A_{n}\cap\{y+1,\ldots,y+hM_{s}\})+(B\cap[T])\right)

\displaystyle\supseteq\bigcup_{y\in E^{\prime}_{n}}\{y+T+1,\ldots,y+hM_{s}\}

\displaystyle=T+E^{\prime}_{n}+[hM_{s}-T].

Let
X_{n}=T+E^{\prime}_{n}+[hM_{s}-T]
, and let
Y_{n}=[H_{s}]\backslash X_{n}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\frac{|X_{n}|}{H_{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H_{s}}\frac{|X_{n}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+hM_{s}\}|}{hM_{s}}+{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{y\in E^{\prime}_{n}}\frac{hM_{s}-T}{hM_{s}}+{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle=qh\alpha+{\rm o}(1).

We will now show that
A+B-n
has density at least
\beta+{\rm o}(1)
in
Y_{n}
for all but
{\rm o}(N_{s})
many
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
.
Since
\lim_{s\to\infty}L_{s}/H_{s}=0
, we have

\left|\big((n+A_{n})+(B\cap[L_{s}])\big)\backslash\big(\left(A+B\right)\cap\{n+1,\ldots,n+H_{s}\}\big)\right|={\rm o}(H_{s}),

so it suffices to compute the density of
A_{n}+(B\cap[L_{s}])
in
Y_{n}
.
Let
W\in\mathbb{N}
be large.
We may write
Y_{n}
as a union of maximal intervals and obtain a decomposition
Y_{n}=Y_{n,1}\cup Y_{n,2}
, where
Y_{n,1}
consists of intervals of length less than
W
in
Y_{n}
and
Y_{n,2}
consists of intervals of length at least
W
in
Y_{n}
.
Since the complement of
Y_{n}
is the set
X_{n}
, which is a union of intervals of length
hM_{s}-T
, we have

\frac{|Y_{n,1}|}{H_{s}}\leqslant\frac{W}{hM_{s}-T+W}={\rm o}(1),

so the set
Y_{n,1}
is negligible.
Now, by construction,
E^{\prime}_{n}
contains a point in every interval of length
L_{s}
in
[H_{s}]
, so the intervals making up
Y_{n,2}
have lengths between
W
and
L_{s}
.
Suppose
\{u+1,\ldots,u+S\}\subseteq Y_{n,2}
is a maximal interval in
Y_{n,2}
.
Then
u\in X_{n}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{n}+(B\cap[T])
, so there exists
v\in A^{\prime}_{n}\subseteq A_{n}
with
u-T\leqslant v\leqslant u
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\left(A_{n}+(B\cap[L_{s}])\right)\cap\right.

\displaystyle\left.\{u+1,\ldots,u+S\}\right|

\displaystyle\geqslant\left|\left(v+(B\cap[L_{s}])\right)\cap\{u+1,\ldots,u+S\}\right|

\displaystyle=|B\cap[L_{s}]\cap\{u-v+1,\ldots,u-v+S\}|

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z},\\
0\leqslant x\leqslant T\end{subarray}}|B\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+S\}|-T.

It follows that the density of
A_{n}+(B\cap[L_{s}])
in
Y_{n,2}
is at least

\frac{\left|\left(A_{n}+(B\cap[L_{s}])\right)\cap Y_{n,2}\right|}{|Y_{n,2}|}\geqslant\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z},\\
0\leqslant x\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathbb{Z}\\
W\leqslant S\leqslant L_{s}\end{subarray}}\frac{|B\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+S\}|}{S}-\frac{T}{W}

Replacing
W
by a slowly growing sequence
W_{s}\to\infty
(for example,
W_{s}=\lfloor\sqrt{M_{s}}\rfloor
), we can retain the property
|Y_{n,1}|={\rm o}(H_{s})
, while also having

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\left(A_{n}+(B\cap[L_{s}])\right)\cap Y_{n,2}\right|}{|Y_{n,2}|}

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z},\\
0\leqslant x\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathbb{Z}\\
W_{s}\leqslant S\leqslant L_{s}\end{subarray}}\frac{|B\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+S\}|}{S}-{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathbb{Z},\\
0\leqslant x\leqslant T\end{subarray}}\underline{d}(B-x)-{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle=\beta-{\rm o}(1).

Therefore,
\underline{d}_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)\geqslant qh\alpha+\beta(1-qh\alpha)=\beta+qh\alpha(1-\beta)
.
Since
\underline{d}_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
by assumption, we conclude
qh\alpha(1-\beta)\leqslant\alpha
.
That is,
\beta\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{qh}
.
Hence,
\beta_{0}=1-h(1-\beta)\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{q}
as claimed.

Claim 3.
\beta_{0}=1-\frac{1}{q}
.

Suppose for contradiction that
\beta_{0}>1-\frac{1}{q}
.
We have

\left|\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})\cap h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+m\}\right|\\
=\left|\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),J_{s})\cap\{\lfloor x/h\rfloor+1,\ldots,\lfloor x/h\rfloor+\lfloor m/h\rfloor\}\right|+{\rm O}(1)

Therefore, by
5.5
, if
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}=(M^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}=(H^{\prime}_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy

\lim_{s\to\infty}|\varepsilon_{s}|M^{\prime}_{s}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lim_{s\to\infty}M^{\prime}_{s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}|\varepsilon_{s}|H^{\prime}_{s}=\infty,

then

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{|\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})\cap h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+H^{\prime}_{s}\}|}{H^{\prime}_{s}}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}\right|={\rm o}(1)

and

\frac{1}{H^{\prime}_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H^{\prime}_{s}}\left|\frac{|\phi_{s}^{-1}(J_{s})\cap h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+M^{\prime}_{s}\}|}{M^{\prime}_{s}}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}\right|\geqslant\frac{\|q\beta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}}{qh}-{\rm o}(1).

(12.1)

Now, by
8.5
(ii)
applied to the function
f=1_{B}-\beta
, let
(V_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\lim_{N\to\infty}V_{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N}{V_{N}}=\infty
such that if
(W_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{W_{N}}{V_{N}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{N}{W_{N}}=\infty
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left|\frac{|B\cap\{n+1,\ldots,n+w_{N}\}|}{W_{N}}-\beta\right|=0.

(12.2)

We adjust the scales
\mathbf{M}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
above to satisfy the additional property

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{M^{\prime}_{s}}{V_{H^{\prime}_{s}}}=\infty.

From the description of the set
A
and the property
\lim_{s\to\infty}|\varepsilon_{s}|H^{\prime}_{s}=\infty
, we see that
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
is a Gowers scale for
A
, so
10.1
still applies at the scale
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
to give a decomposition of
B\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H^{\prime}_{s}-1\}
.
Thus, by (
12.1
),

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{\prime}_{s}}\sum_{h=1}^{H^{\prime}_{s}}\left|\frac{|B\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+M^{\prime}_{s}\}|}{M^{\prime}_{s}}-\beta\right|\\
=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{\prime}_{s}}\sum_{h=1}^{H^{\prime}_{s}}\left|\frac{|B_{s,0}\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+M^{\prime}_{s}\}|}{M^{\prime}_{s}}-\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}\right|\geqslant\frac{\|q\beta_{0}\|_{\mathbb{T}}}{qh}>0.

This contradicts (
12.2
).

A consequence of Claim 3 is that
q\geqslant 2
(since
\beta_{0}>0
).

Now, as in the proof of
11.1
, we may find
n_{s}\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
with
n_{s}={\rm o}(N_{s})
such that
|((A-n_{s})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\})\triangle A_{n}|={\rm o}(H_{s})
and then apply
9.1
to find, for all but
{\rm o}(N_{s})
many
m\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
, a number
Q_{m}\in h\mathbb{N}
with
H_{s}\leqslant Q_{m}\leqslant(1+{\rm o}(1))H_{s}
and sets
\tilde{A}_{m}\subseteq(A-n_{s}+a_{n_{s}})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\}
and
\tilde{B}_{m}\subseteq(B-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\}
such that

(I)

|(A-n_{s}+a_{n_{s}})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\}\backslash\tilde{A}_{m}|={\rm o}(Q_{m})
,

(II)

|(B-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\}\backslash\tilde{B}_{m}|={\rm o}(Q_{m})
,

(III)

|(\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{n})\backslash((A+B-n_{s}+a_{n_{s}}-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\})|={\rm o}(Q_{m})
, and

(IV)

\tilde{A}_{m}\subseteq h\mathbb{Z}\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\}
.

Note that by
(I)
,

|\tilde{A}_{m}\triangle(A_{n_{s}}+a_{n_{s}})|={\rm o}(Q_{m}).

The set
\frac{A_{n_{s}}+a_{n_{s}}}{h}=\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})
has positive density in every residue class mod
d\leqslant D_{s}
for some
D_{s}\to\infty
.
Indeed, for
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\}
, we may write

\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})\cap(d\mathbb{Z}+r)=d\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(dh(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})+r,

and this set has positive density in every interval of length large compared with
d|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}
by
5.5
.
Thus, we can take, for example,
D_{s}=\lfloor\sqrt{|\varepsilon_{s}|H_{s}}\rfloor
.
Since the intersection
\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})\cap\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor H_{s}/h\rfloor-1\}\cap(d\mathbb{Z}+r)
has positive density in
\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor H_{s}/h\rfloor-1\}
, we conclude that
\tilde{A}_{m}\cap(hd\mathbb{Z}+hr)\neq\emptyset
for every
d\leqslant D_{s}
and
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\}
.

By
(III)
and the assumption
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
, we must have

|\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{m}|\leqslant(\alpha+\beta+{\rm o}(1))Q_{m}

for all but
{\rm o}(1)
many
m\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
.
We can therefore apply the inverse theorem for sumsets in finite cyclic groups to deduce structural information about
\tilde{A}_{m}
and
\tilde{B}_{m}
.

As preparation for applying the inverse theorem, we decompose
\tilde{B}_{m}=\bigcup_{j=0}^{h-1}\tilde{B}_{m,j}
with
\tilde{B}_{m,j}=\{x\in\tilde{B}_{m}:x\equiv j\pmod{h}\}
.
Using
(II)
, we have
|\tilde{B}_{m,j}|\geqslant(\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}-{\rm o}(1))Q_{m}=\frac{1}{h}\left(1-\frac{1}{q}-{\rm o}(1)\right)Q_{m}
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
.
Let
A^{\prime}_{m}=\frac{\tilde{A}_{m}}{h}
and
B^{\prime}_{m,j}=\frac{\tilde{B}_{m,j}-j}{h}
.
We then have

|\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{m}|=\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\left|A^{\prime}_{m}+_{\bmod{\frac{Q_{m}}{h}}}B^{\prime}_{m,j}\right|.

But
A^{\prime}_{m}
has nonempty intersection with every subgroup of index at most
D_{s}/h
, so by
4.4
,

\frac{|\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{m}|}{Q_{m}}\geqslant\frac{1}{h}\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\min\{\alpha_{0}+\beta_{m,j},1\}-{\rm o}(1),

where
\beta_{m,j}=\frac{B^{\prime}_{m,j}}{Q_{m}/h}
.
Combined with the inequality
\frac{|\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{m}|}{Q_{m}}\leqslant\alpha+\beta+{\rm o}(1)
,
11.2
implies that
\min_{0\leqslant j\leqslant h-1}\beta_{m,j}=1-\frac{1}{q}+{\rm o}(1)
.
Let
j_{m}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
such that
\beta_{m,j_{m}}=1-\frac{1}{q}+{\rm o}(1)
.
For
j\neq j_{m}
, we then have
\beta_{m,j}=1-{\rm o}(1)
since
\frac{1}{h}\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\beta_{m,j}=\beta+{\rm o}(1)=1-\frac{1}{qh}+{\rm o}(1)
.
We can now update our bound on

\left|A^{\prime}_{m}+_{\bmod{\frac{Q_{m}}{h}}}B^{\prime}_{m,j}\right|

by

\left|A^{\prime}_{m}+_{\bmod{\frac{Q_{m}}{h}}}B^{\prime}_{m,j}\right|=(1-{\rm o}(1))\frac{Q_{m}}{h}

for
j\neq j_{m}
and

\left|A^{\prime}_{m}+_{\bmod{\frac{Q_{m}}{h}}}B^{\prime}_{m,j_{m}}\right|=(\alpha_{0}+\beta_{m,j_{m}}+{\rm o}(1))\frac{Q_{m}}{h}.

(The lower bound comes from
4.4
and the upper bound is then imposed by the condition
|\tilde{A}_{m}+_{\bmod{Q_{m}}}\tilde{B}_{m}|\leqslant(\alpha+\beta+{\rm o}(1))Q_{m}
.)

We may now apply
4.6
to conclude that

|B^{\prime}_{m,j_{m}}\triangle(\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s},J^{\prime}_{m}))\cap\{0,1,\ldots,Q_{m}-1\})|={\rm o}(Q_{m})

for some interval
J^{\prime}_{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}
of length
1-\frac{1}{q}
.
(The given structure of
A^{\prime}_{m}
as agreeing up to zero density with a Bohr set precludes the other possibilities enumerated in
4.6
.)

We now finish by arguing as in Claim 1.
As we have shown, the set
(B-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}
has density
1-{\rm o}(1)
in
h-1
residue classes mod
h
.
In the remaining residue class,
(B-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\}
is approximately equal to a translate of
h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),J^{\prime}_{m})
.
Using the description of
A
, we may choose
x,y\in A
such that
x\equiv y\pmod{h}
and
x\not\equiv y\pmod{qh}
.
(It is important here that
q\geqslant 2
.)
Since the interval
J^{\prime}_{m}
has length
|J^{\prime}_{m}|=1-\frac{1}{q}-{\rm o}(1)
, the set

\{x,y\}+h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),J^{\prime}_{m})

has density
1-{\rm o}(1)
in the multiples of
h
in every interval whose length is long compared with
|\varepsilon_{s}|^{-1}
.
Since
|\varepsilon_{s}|H_{s}\to\infty
, we thus have

|\{x,y\}+((B-m)\cap\{0,1,\ldots,H_{s}-1\})|=(1-{\rm o}(1))H_{s},

so
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)\geqslant d_{\mathbf{N}}(\{x,y\}+B)=1
.
This is a contradiction, and the proof is complete.
∎

13.  Local ergodicity

We will now use the description of the sets
A
and
B
in case
(1’)
of
11.4
(with
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
, as provided by
12.1
) to deduce that
1_{A}-\alpha
and
1_{B}-\beta
are locally ergodic.
Our argument relies on the estimates from
Section

5
.

Throughout this section, we fix sets
A
and
B
satisfying
(1’)
in
11.4
for a given frequency
\theta
.

Proposition 13.1
.

If
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
, then
1_{A}-\alpha
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
.
Moreover, if
q\in\mathbb{Q}\backslash\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{h}
, then
n\mapsto 1_{A}(n+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A}(n+t_{k})e(nq)
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

For
s\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
for which we have local structural information about
A
at
n
, let
A_{n}=(\phi_{s}^{-1}(I_{n})\cap h\mathbb{Z})-a_{n}=h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})-a_{n}
.

Fix
Q\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\delta(Q)=\min_{1\leqslant q\leqslant Q}\left\|q\theta\right\|>0

and

\min_{1\leqslant q\leqslant Q}\left\|q(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})\right\|\geqslant\frac{\delta(Q)}{2}

for all sufficiently large
s
.
Hence, if
M\geqslant\frac{Q\log{Q}}{\delta(Q)}
, then by Lemma
5.3
and the estimate
|I_{n}|=\alpha_{0}+{\rm o}(1)=h\alpha+{\rm o}(1)
from above, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\Bigg|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}

\displaystyle 1_{A_{n}}(x+m)-\alpha\Bigg|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{|(h\cdot\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n}))\cap\{x+1,\ldots,x+M\}|}{M}-\alpha\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{|\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})\cap\{y+1,\ldots,y+\lfloor M/h\rfloor\}|}{M}-\alpha\right|+{\rm O}\left(\frac{1}{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\cdot\sup_{y\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{|\mathrm{Bohr}(h(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}),I_{n})\cap\{y+1,\ldots,y+\lfloor M/h\rfloor\}|}{\lfloor M/h\rfloor}-h\alpha\right|+{\rm O}\left(\frac{1}{M}\right)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{Q}+\frac{\log{Q}}{\lfloor M/h\rfloor\delta(Q)/2}+\frac{1}{M}+{\rm o}(1)

\displaystyle\ll\frac{1}{Q}+{\rm o}(1).

Therefore,

\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{A}(n+m)-\alpha\right|=\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H_{s}}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{A}(n+m+x)-\alpha\right|+{\rm O}\left(\frac{H_{s}}{N_{s}}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H_{s}}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{A_{n}}(x+m)-\alpha\right|+{\rm o}(1)\ll\frac{1}{Q}+{\rm o}(1).

Taking a limit first as
s\to\infty
and then as
Q\to\infty
, we have

\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{A}(n+m)-\alpha\right|=0.

That is,
1_{A}-\alpha
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
q\in\mathbb{Q}\backslash\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{h}
.
Similarly to the above, we may approximate

f(m)=1_{A}(m+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A}(m+t_{k})e(mq)

locally (for
m\in\{n,n+1,\ldots,n+H_{s}-1\}
with
n\in\{N_{s-1}+1,\ldots,N_{s}\}
) by

f_{n}(m)=1_{A_{n}}(m-n+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A_{n}}(m-n+t_{k})e((m-n)q)e(nq).

We can write

f_{n}(n-a_{n}+x)=e((n-a_{n})q)e(xq)\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{h\mathbb{N}}(x+t_{j})1_{I_{n}}((x+t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})).

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
be minimal such that
dq\in\mathbb{Z}
.
By assumption,
d\nmid h
.
Given
M\in\mathbb{N}
, we may decompose
[M]
into residue classes mod
d
to estimate

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}f_{n}(n-a_{n}+x+m)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/d}f_{n}(n-a_{n}+x+md+r)\right|+{\rm O}\left(\frac{d}{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}e(rq)\mathcal{E}_{r}(x,M/d)\right|+{\rm O}\left(\frac{d}{M}\right)

for
x\in\{a_{n},a_{n}+1,\ldots,a_{n}+H_{s}-M-1\}
,
where

\mathcal{E}_{r}(M/d)=\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/d}\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{h\mathbb{N}}(x+md+r+t_{j})1_{I_{n}}((x+md+r+t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})).

If there exist
j_{1},j_{2}
such that
t_{j_{1}}\not\equiv t_{j_{2}}\pmod{h}
, then

1_{h\mathbb{N}}(x+md+r+t_{j_{1}})1_{h\mathbb{N}}(x+md+r+t_{j_{2}})=0

for all
m
, so
\mathcal{E}_{r}(x,M/d)=0
.
Suppose
t_{j}\equiv t\pmod{h}
for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Then we can write

\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{h\mathbb{N}}(x+md+r+t_{j})=1_{h\mathbb{N}-r-t-x}(md)

Let
\ell=\gcd(d,h)
and
d^{\prime}=\frac{d}{\ell}
and
h^{\prime}=\frac{h}{\ell}
.
Since
d\nmid h
, we have
d^{\prime}\geqslant 2
.
Also,

1_{h\mathbb{N}-r-t-x}(md)=\begin{cases}1_{h^{\prime}\mathbb{N}-\frac{r-t-x}{\ell}}(md^{\prime}),&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell\mid r-t;\\
0,&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell\nmid r-t\end{cases}

Therefore, if
\ell\nmid r-t-x
, we have
\mathcal{E}_{r}(x,M/d)=0
.
Let
r\in\{0,1,\ldots,d-1\}
such that
\ell\mid r-t-x
.
Then taking
r^{\prime}\equiv\frac{r-t-x}{\ell}(d^{\prime})^{-1}\pmod{h^{\prime}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{r}(M/d)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/d}1_{h^{\prime}\mathbb{N}-\frac{r-t-x}{\ell}}(md^{\prime})\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{I_{n}}((x+md+r+t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/d}1_{h^{\prime}\mathbb{N}-r^{\prime}}(m)\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{I_{n}}((x+md+r+t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/(dh^{\prime})}\prod_{j=1}^{k}1_{I_{n}}((x+mdh^{\prime}-dr^{\prime}+r+t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}))+{\rm O}\left(\frac{dh^{\prime}}{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{M/d}\sum_{m=1}^{M/(dh^{\prime})}1_{I_{n,r}}(x+mdh^{\prime}(\theta+\varepsilon_{s}))+{\rm O}\left(\frac{dh^{\prime}}{M}\right),

where

I_{n,r}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}\left(I_{n}+(dr^{\prime}-r-t_{j})(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})\right).

Note that if we let

I^{\prime}_{n}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}(I_{n}-t_{j}(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})),

then
I_{n,r}=I^{\prime}_{n}+(dr^{\prime}-r)(\theta+\varepsilon_{s})
, so the length
|I_{n,r}|=|I^{\prime}_{n}|
does not depend on
r
.
Applying
5.3
as above with
M\geqslant\frac{Q\log{Q}}{\delta(Q)}
, we have

\left|\mathcal{E}_{r}(x,M/d)-h^{\prime}|I^{\prime}_{n}|\right|\ll\frac{1}{Q}.

Putting everything together,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}f_{n}(n-a_{n}+m)\right|

\displaystyle\ll\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r=0}^{d-1}e(rq)\mathcal{E}_{r}(M/d)\right|+\frac{1}{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ell}\left|\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{d^{\prime}-1}e(r^{\prime}\ell q)\mathcal{E}_{r^{\prime}\ell+t}(M/d)\right|+\frac{1}{M}

\displaystyle\ll\frac{h^{\prime}|I^{\prime}_{n}|}{\ell}\left|\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{d^{\prime}-1}e(r^{\prime}\ell q)\right|+\frac{1}{Q}.

But
\ell q
is a rational number with denominator
d^{\prime}\geqslant 2
, so
\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{d^{\prime}-1}e(r^{\prime}\ell q)=0
.
Thus,

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}f(n+m)\right|=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{s}}\sum_{x=1}^{H_{s}}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}f_{n}(n-a_{n}+x+m)\right|\ll\frac{1}{Q},

so
f
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
.
∎

Proposition 13.2
.

If
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
, then
1_{B}-\beta
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
.

Proof.

By
13.1
, every
U^{1}
good scale for
A
satisfies
\|1_{A}-\alpha\|_{U^{1}(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{H})}=0
, so we may freely adjust the scale
\mathbf{H}
.
By taking
\mathbf{K}=(K_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
to be sufficiently quickly growing and letting
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}=\mathbf{N}_{\mathbf{K}}
, the pair
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{N})
is a
U^{2}
good scale for
A
by
8.9
.
Letting this scale play the role of
(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{H})
, we consider the resulting decomposition

B\cap[N_{s}]=(B_{s,0}\cup B_{s,1}\cup F_{s})-b_{0}

as provided by
10.1
.
(The pair
(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{H})
is not necessarily a Gowers scale for
B

a priori
, so we cannot apply
11.4
and instead use
10.1
.)
We have

\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{B}(n+m)-\beta\right|\\
\leqslant\underbrace{\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{B_{s,0}}(n+m)-\frac{\beta_{0}}{h}\right|}_{[1]}\\
+\underbrace{\limsup_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}\left|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}1_{B_{s,1}}(n+m)-\left(1-\frac{1}{h}\right)\right|}_{[2]}.

The term [1] is equal to zero by the same argument as in the proof of
13.1
using
\theta\notin\mathbb{Q}
, and [2] is also zero because
|([N_{s}]\backslash h\mathbb{N})\backslash B_{s,1}|={\rm o}(N_{s})
.
∎

14.  Completing the proof with ergodic theory

Combining the results of the preceding sections, we can prove
3.2
, reproduced below.

3.2
.

Let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
d(A)=\alpha>0
and
d(B)=\beta>0
such that
\alpha+\beta<1
.
Suppose
B
meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence with
\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s}=\infty
such that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
.
Then there exists a subsequence
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
of
\mathbf{N}
such that either

(1)

1_{A}-\alpha
and
1_{B}-\beta
are locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
and there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
such that
n\mapsto 1_{A}(n+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A}(n+t_{k})e(nq)
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
for every
q\in\mathbb{Q}\backslash\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{h}
, every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and every
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, or

(2)

there exist integers
h\geqslant 2
and
a_{0},b_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
such that
A\subseteq h\mathbb{N}-a_{0}
,
A+h\sim_{\mathbf{N}^{\prime}}A
, and
B\sim_{\mathbf{N}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})-b_{0}
.

Proof.

By
8.5
, let
\mathbf{H}_{G,A}
and
\mathbf{H}_{G,B}
be Gowers scales for
A
and
B
respectively.
Define
\mathbf{H}_{G}=(H_{G,s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
by
H_{G,s}=\max\{H_{G,A,s},H_{G,B,s}\}
.
Then by
8.7
, there is a subsequence
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{H}_{G}^{\prime})
of
(\mathbf{N},\mathbf{H}_{G})
such that there exist scales
\mathbf{H}^{-},\mathbf{H}^{+}
such that
\mathbf{H}_{G}^{\prime}\preceq\mathbf{H}^{-}\prec\mathbf{H}^{+}\preceq\mathbf{N}^{\prime}
and for every
\mathbf{H}
with
\mathbf{H}^{-}\prec\mathbf{H}\prec\mathbf{H}^{+}
, the pair
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{H})
is a
U^{2}
good scale for
A
.
Applying
8.7
again, after replacing
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{H}^{+},\mathbf{H}^{-})
by a subsequence, there exists
\mathbf{H}
with
\mathbf{H}^{-}\prec\mathbf{H}\prec\mathbf{H}^{+}
such that
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{H})
is a
U^{2}
good scale for
B
.
Hence,
(\mathbf{N}^{\prime},\mathbf{H})
is a
U^{2}
good scale and a Gowers scale for both
A
and
B
.

By passing to a further subsequence, we may assume
\lim_{s\to\infty}N^{\prime}_{s}/N^{\prime}_{s+1}=0
.
Now by
11.4
, there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
such that either
(1’)
or
(2’)
holds.
The property
(2’)
gives conclusion
(2)
.
Suppose
(1’)
holds.
Then by
12.1
, the frequency
\theta
is irrational.

13.1
and
13.2
then imply that
(1)
holds.
∎

In order to prove
1.4
, it remains only to prove the following, which is a restatement of
3.3
.

Theorem 14.1
.

Let
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
d(A)=\alpha>0
and
d(B)=\beta>0
such that
\alpha+\beta<1
.
Suppose
B
meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be an increasing sequence with
\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s}/N_{s+1}=0
such that
d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
.
Suppose
1_{A}-\alpha
and
1_{B}-\beta
are locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
and there exists
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
such that
n\mapsto 1_{A}(n+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A}(n+t_{k})e(nq)
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
for every
q\in\mathbb{Q}\backslash\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{h}
, every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and every
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then there exist
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{0},b_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
, an irrational
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, and closed intervals
I,J\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that if

A_{0}=A+a_{0},\hskip 10.00002ptB_{0}=(B+b_{0})\cap h\mathbb{N},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptB_{1}=(B+b_{0})\backslash h\mathbb{N},

then
A_{0}\subseteq h\mathbb{N}
,
B_{1}\sim\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N}
,
A_{0}\sim\{n\in h\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in I\}
, and
B_{0}\sim\{n\in h\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in J\}
.

The local ergodicity assumption on
1_{A}-\alpha
and
1_{B}-\beta
enables us to transfer the problem into a dynamical setting, where we apply tools from ergodic theory.
For an overview of this strategy, see
Section

3
.

14.1.  Dynamical model

As a first step in the dynamical approach to proving
14.1
, we produce dynamical systems modeling the sets
A
,
B
, and
A+B
.

Given an invertible measure-preserving system
(X,\mu,T)
, a measurable subset
E\subseteq X
, and a set
D\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, we define the sumset
D+E
by

D+E=\bigcup_{d\in D}T^{d}E.

Theorem 14.2
.

There exist ergodic invertible measure-preserving systems
(X_{A},\mu_{A},T_{A})
and
(X_{B},\mu_{B},T_{B})
, transitive points
x_{A}\in X_{A}
and
x_{B}\in X_{B}
, clopen sets
E_{A}\subseteq X_{A}
and
E_{B}\subseteq X_{B}
, and continuous factor maps
\pi_{A}:X_{A}\to G
and
\pi_{B}:X_{B}\to G
, where
(G,m,\theta)
is the maximal common rotational factor, such that:

(1)

\pi_{A}(x_{A})=\pi_{B}(x_{B})=0
,

(2)

A=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:T_{A}^{n}x_{A}\in E_{A}\}
and
B=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:T_{B}^{n}x_{B}\in E_{B}\}
,

(3)

\mu_{A}(E_{A})=\alpha
and
\mu_{B}(E_{B})=\beta
,

(4)

\max\{\mu_{A}(B+E_{A}),\mu_{B}(A+E_{B})\}\leqslant\alpha+\beta
, and

(5)

the number
C_{G}
of connected components of
G
satisfies
C_{G}<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
.

We prove
14.2
through a sequence of lemmas.
To begin, we have the following version of the Furstenberg correspondence principle.

Lemma 14.3
.

Let
C\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be a set with positive natural density
d(C)=\gamma>0
.
Let
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence along which
C
admits correlations.
Suppose
1_{C}-\gamma
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
.
Then there exists a topological dynamical system
(Y,S)
, a clopen subset
F\subseteq Y
, a transitive point
c\in Y
, and an
S
-invariant measure
\nu
such that
c\in\textup{gen}(\nu,\mathbf{N})
with the following properties:

(1)

C=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:S^{n}c\in F\}
.

(2)

\nu(F)=\gamma
.

(3)

for any set
D\subseteq\mathbb{N}
,

\nu(D+F)\leqslant\underline{d}_{\mathbf{N}}(D+C).

(4)

\mathbb{E}\left[1_{F}\mid\mathcal{I}\right]=\gamma
.

Proof.

We let
(Y,\nu,S)
be the Furstenberg system of
C
along
\mathbf{N}
(see
Section

7.4
).
Let
\Phi:\mathcal{A}_{C}\to C(Y)
be the Gelfand representation, where
\mathcal{A}_{C}\subseteq\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
is the translation-invariant *-algebra generated by
1_{C}
, and let
F
be the clopen set defined by
1_{F}=\Phi(1_{C})
.
Let
c\in Y
be the point such that
\Phi(f)(c)=f(0)
for
f\in\mathcal{A}_{C}
.
Then by the definition of
\nu
, we have for every
f\in C(Y)
,

\int_{Y}f\penalty\ d\nu=\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}(\Phi^{-1}(f))(n)=\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}f(S^{n}c),

so
\nu=\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}\delta_{S^{n}c}
.
That is,
c\in\textup{gen}(\nu,\mathbf{N})
.
Let us check that properties
(1)
–
(4)
hold.

(1)
By the choice of the set
F
and the point
c
, we have

\{n\in\mathbb{N}:S^{n}c\in F\}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:1_{F}(S^{n}c)=1\}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:1_{C}(n)=1\}=C.

(2)
By the definition of the measure
\nu
and the identity
1_{F}=\Phi(1_{C})
, we may compute

\nu(F)=\int_{Y}\Phi(1_{C})\penalty\ d\nu=\mathbb{E}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}1_{C}(n)=d_{\mathbf{N}}(C)=d(C)=\gamma.

(3)
Note that

D+C=\bigcup_{d\in D}\{d+n:S^{n}c\in F\}=\bigcup_{d\in D}\{n:S^{n}c\in S^{d}F\}=\{n:S^{n}c\in D+F\}.

The set
D+F
is open (it is a union of open sets), so by the portmanteau lemma,

\underline{d}_{\mathbf{N}}(D+C)=\liminf_{s\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{s}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{s}}1_{D+F}(S^{n}c)\geqslant\nu(D+F).

(4)
This follows from Theorem
7.13
and the assumption that
1_{C}-\gamma
is locally ergodic.
∎

We apply Lemma
14.3
to obtain systems
(Y_{A},\nu_{A},S_{A})
and
(Y_{B},\nu_{B},S_{B})
, points
a\in Y_{A}
and
b\in Y_{B}
, and sets
F_{A}\subseteq Y_{A}
and
F_{B}\subseteq Y_{B}
corresponding to
A
and
B
respectively.
Applying property
(3)
with
D\in\{A,B\}
, we have

\max\{\nu_{A}(B+F_{A}),\nu_{B}(A+F_{B})\}\leqslant d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta.

(14.1)

We now consider the ergodic decomposition of the measure
\nu_{A}
, which we may write as

\nu_{A}=\int_{Y_{A}}\nu_{A,y}\penalty\ d\nu_{A}(y),

where
\nu_{A,y}
is
\sigma
-invariant and ergodic for
\nu_{A}
-almost every
y\in Y_{A}
, and

\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{A}}\left[f\mid\mathcal{I}\right](y)=\int_{Y_{A}}f\penalty\ d\nu_{A,y}

for every
f\in L^{1}(\nu_{A})
and
\nu_{A}
-almost every
y\in Y_{A}
.
By (
14.1
),

\int_{Y_{A}}\nu_{A,y}(B+F_{A})\penalty\ d\nu_{A}(y)=\nu_{A}(B+F_{A})\leqslant\alpha+\beta,

so the set

G_{1}=\left\{y:\nu_{A,y}(B+F_{A})\leqslant\alpha+\beta\right\}

satisfies
\nu_{A}(G_{1})>0
.
Moreover, by property
(4)
from Lemma
14.3
,

\nu_{A,y}(F_{A})=\int_{Y_{A}}1_{F_{A}}\penalty\ d\nu_{A,y}=\mathbb{E}_{\nu_{A}}\left[1_{F_{A}}\mid\mathcal{I}\right](y)=\alpha

for
\nu_{A}
-almost every
y\in Y_{A}
.
That is, the set

G_{2}=\left\{y:\nu_{A,y}(F_{A})=\alpha\right\}

has full measure.
Finally, let
G_{3}
be the full measure set of
y\in Y_{A}
such that
\nu_{A,y}
is ergodic.
Choose
y\in G_{1}\cap G_{2}\cap G_{3}
.
Then
\nu_{A,y}
is an ergodic
S_{A}
-invariant measure with
\nu_{A,y}(F_{A})=\alpha
and
\nu_{A,y}(B+F_{A})\leqslant\alpha+\beta
.
We can repeat the same argument to find an
S_{B}
-invariant ergodic measure
\nu_{B,y^{\prime}}
such that
\nu_{B,y^{\prime}}(F_{B})=\beta
and
\nu_{B,y^{\prime}}(A+F_{B})\leqslant\alpha+\beta
.

We will now use Lemma
7.4
to replace
(Y_{A},\nu_{A,y},S_{A})
and
(Y_{B},\nu_{B,y^{\prime}},S_{B})
by more convenient extensions.
Let
(G,m,\theta)
be the maximal common rotational factor of the system
(Y_{A},\nu_{A,y},S_{A})
and
(Y_{B},\nu_{B,y^{\prime}},S_{B})
.
By Lemma
7.4
, let
(X_{A},\mu_{A},T_{A})
be an ergodic system,
x_{A}\in X_{A}
a transitive point, and
\rho:X_{A}\to Y_{A}
a continuous factor map such that
\rho(x_{A})=a
,
\rho
is a measurable isomorphism, and there exists a continuous factor map
\pi_{A}:X_{A}\to G
.
By conjugating the factor map
\pi_{A}
, we may assume for convenience that
\pi_{A}(x_{A})=0
.
We then let
E_{A}=\rho^{-1}(F_{A})
.
Define an extension
(X_{B},\mu_{B},T_{B})
of
(Y_{B},\nu_{B,y^{\prime}},S_{B})
similarly.
To complete the proof of
14.2
, it remains to show that the number
C_{G}
of connected components of
G
satisfies
C_{G}<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
.

Lemma 14.4
.

For
\nu_{A}
-almost every
y\in Y_{A}
, the set of eigenvalues

\sigma_{y}=\{t\in\mathbb{T}:\exists f\in L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})\penalty\ \text{such that}\penalty\ |f|=1\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ S_{A}f=e(t)f\}

satisfies
\sigma_{y}\cap\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\left\{0,\frac{1}{h},\ldots,\frac{h-1}{h}\right\}
.

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{Q}\backslash\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{h}
.
By assumption, the function
n\mapsto 1_{A}(n+t_{1})\ldots 1_{A}(n+t_{k})e(-nq)
is locally ergodic along
\mathbf{N}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, for every
u\in\mathcal{A}_{A}
, we have that
n\mapsto u(n)e(-nq)
is locally ergodic.

Suppose for contradiction that
\nu_{A}(\{y\in Y_{A}:q\in\sigma_{y}\})>0
.
Fix a countable dense set
D\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{A}
.
Let
f\in L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})
with
|f|=1
such that
S_{A}f=e(q)f
.
The space
C(Y_{A})
is dense in
L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})
, so for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
u\in D
such that
\|f-\Phi(u)\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})}<\varepsilon
.
Therefore,

\|\Phi(\tau^{m}u)-\Phi(e(mq)u)\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})}<2\varepsilon

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
By countable additivity of the measure
\nu_{A}
, there exists
u\in D
such that the set

S_{u}=\{y\in Y_{A}:\|\Phi(u)\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})}>1-\varepsilon\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \|\Phi(\tau^{m}u)-\Phi(e(mq)u)\|_{L^{2}(\nu_{A,y})}<2\varepsilon\penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{N}\}

has
\nu_{A}(S_{u})>0
.

Let
\tilde{u}(n)=u(n)e(-nq)
.
The function
\tilde{u}
may not belong to the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{A}
, so let
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
be the translation-invariant *-algebra generated by
\mathcal{A}_{A}
and
n\mapsto e(-nq)
.
Let
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{\nu},\tilde{S})
be the Furstenberg system of
\tilde{A}
along
\mathbf{N}
, and let
\tilde{\Phi}:\tilde{\mathcal{A}}\to C(\tilde{Y})
be the Gelfand representation.
There is a factor map
\pi:\tilde{Y}\to Y
given by
\pi(\tilde{y})=\tilde{y}|_{\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{A}}
when viewing points
\tilde{y}\in\tilde{Y}
as C*-algebra homomorphisms
\tilde{y}:\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{A}}}\to\mathbb{C}
.

For convenience, let
e_{q}(n)=e(-nq)
so that
\tilde{u}=ue_{q}
.
We have

(\tau^{m}\tilde{u})(n)=\tilde{u}(n+m)=e(-mq)(\tau^{m}u)(n)e(-nq)=e(-mq)(e_{q}\tau^{m}u)(n),

so

\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tau^{m}\tilde{u})-\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u})\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}<2\varepsilon

for
y\in S_{u}
and any measure
\rho
on
\tilde{Y}
with
\pi_{*}\rho=\nu_{A,y}
.
If we let
(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})_{\tilde{y}\in\tilde{Y}}
be an ergodic decomposition of
\tilde{\nu}
, then
\pi_{*}\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}}=\nu_{A,\pi(\tilde{y})}
for
\tilde{\nu}
-almost every
\tilde{y}\in\tilde{Y}
.
Therefore,

\tilde{S}_{u}=\left\{\tilde{y}\in\tilde{Y}:\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}>1-\varepsilon\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \|\tilde{\Phi}(\tau^{m}\tilde{u})-\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}<2\varepsilon\penalty\ \text{for all}\penalty\ m\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

satisfies
\tilde{\nu}(\pi^{-1}(S_{u})\backslash\tilde{S}_{u})=0
, so
\tilde{\nu}(S_{u})>0
.
By the discrete mean ergodic theorem (
7.11
),

\lim_{M\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\tau^{m}\tilde{u}-\tilde{u}_{\mathrm{inv}}\right\|_{2,\mathbf{N}}=0.

Hence, if
\tilde{y}\in\tilde{S}_{u}
, we have

\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u}_{\mathrm{erg}})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}=\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u})-\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u}_{\mathrm{inv}})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}\leqslant 2\varepsilon.

On the other hand,
\tilde{u}
is locally ergodic by assumption, so

\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u}_{\mathrm{erg}})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}=\|\tilde{\Phi}(\tilde{u})\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{\nu}_{\tilde{y}})}>1-\varepsilon

for
\tilde{\nu}
-almost every
\tilde{y}\in\tilde{S}_{u}
.
If
\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
, this gives a contradiction.
∎

We can now combine the results of this section to prove
14.2
.

Proof of
14.2
.

Let
(X_{A},\mu_{A},T_{A})
and
(X_{B},\mu_{B},T_{B})
be the systems defined above.
By construction, we have transitive points
x_{A}\in X_{A}
and
x_{B}\in X_{B}
and continuous factor maps
\pi_{A}:X_{A}\to G
and
\pi_{B}:X_{B}\to G
satisfying
\pi_{A}(x_{A})=\pi_{B}(x_{B})=0
.

By
14.3
,

A=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:S_{A}^{n}y_{A}\in F_{A}\}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:T_{A}^{n}x_{A}\in E_{A}\}

and similarly
B=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:T_{B}^{n}x_{B}\in E_{B}\}
.

The ergodic measures
\mu_{A}
and
\mu_{B}
were chosen so that
\mu_{A}(E_{A})=\alpha
,
\mu_{B}(E_{B})=\beta
,
\mu_{A}(B+E_{A})\leqslant\alpha+\beta
and
\mu_{B}(A+E_{B})\leqslant\alpha+\beta
.

Finally, by
14.4
we may choose
\mu_{A}
so that

\sigma_{A}=\{t\in\mathbb{T}:\exists f\in L^{2}(\mu_{A})\penalty\ \text{such that}\penalty\ |f|=1\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ T_{A}f=e(t)f\}

satisfies
\sigma_{A}\cap\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\left\{0,\frac{1}{h},\ldots,\frac{h-1}{h}\right\}
.
By the Halmos–von Neumann theorem (see
[
16
, p. 48]
), the Kronecker factor
(Z_{A},m_{Z_{A}},\theta_{A})
of
(X_{A},\mu_{A},T_{A})
is (isomorphic to) a rotation on the compact dual group of
\sigma_{A}
when viewing
\sigma_{A}
as a discrete group.
The fact that all torsion elements of
\sigma_{A}
are of the form
\frac{k}{h}
for some
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,h-1\}
implies that
Z_{A}=\hat{\sigma}_{A}
has at most
h
connected components.
Since
(G,m,\theta)
is a factor of
(Z_{A},m_{Z_{A}},\theta_{A})
, we conclude that
C_{G}\leqslant h<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
.
∎

14.2.  Reducing to the rotational factor

Our next step is to replace the dynamical sumsets
B+E_{A}
and
A+E_{B}
with convolutions of functions on the compact group
G
, which will allow us to reduce
14.1
to
1.2
.

Let
\varphi_{A}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[1_{E_{A}}\mid G]
and
\varphi_{B}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[1_{E_{B}}\mid G]
be the projections of
1_{E_{A}}
and
1_{E_{B}}
onto
G
.
Consider the set
S=\{x\in G:(\varphi_{A}*\varphi_{B})(x)>0\}
.
The following proposition describes the relationship between
S
and the sets
A+E_{B}
and
B+E_{A}
.
The notation
U\subseteq_{\mu}V
means
\mu(U\backslash V)=0
.

Proposition 14.5
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

\int_{G}\varphi_{A}\penalty\ dm=\mu_{A}(E_{A})=\alpha
and
\int_{G}\varphi_{B}\penalty\ dm=\mu_{B}(E_{B})=\beta
,

(2)

E_{A}\subseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}\left(\{\varphi_{A}>0\}\right)
and
E_{B}\subseteq_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}\left(\{\varphi_{B}>0\}\right)
,

(3)

B+E_{A}\supseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(S)
and
A+E_{B}\supseteq_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(S)
, and

(4)

m(S)\leqslant\alpha+\beta
.

We carry out the proof in stages.
First, we recall a fundamental tool in the analysis of multiple ergodic averages: the van der Corput lemma.

Lemma 14.6

(
[
1
, Theorem 1.5]
)
.

Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a bounded sequence in a Hilbert space.
If the limit

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\langle u_{n+h},u_{n}\right\rangle

exists for every
h\in\mathbb{N}
and

\limsup_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left|\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left\langle u_{n+h},u_{n}\right\rangle\right|=0,

then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}u_{n}\right\|=0.

Now, we utilize the van der Corput lemma to show that certain bilinear averages can be reduced to convolutions on
G
.

Lemma 14.7
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{A})
and
g\in L^{2}(\mu_{B})
.
Then for
(\mu_{A}\times\mu_{B})
-a.e.
(x_{1},x_{2})\in X_{A}\times X_{B}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(T^{n}x_{1})g(T^{-n}x_{2})=(\tilde{f}*\tilde{g})(\pi_{A}(x_{1})+\pi_{B}(x_{2})),

where
\tilde{f}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid G]
and
\tilde{g}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid G]
are the projections of
f
and
g
onto
G
.

Proof.

The average on the left hand side converges almost everywhere by the pointwise ergodic theorem.
For ease of computation, we will establish equality with the right hand side in
L^{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}_{A}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{B}
be the Kronecker factors of
(X_{A},\mu_{A},T_{A})
and
(X_{B},\mu,T_{B})
respectively.
We break the proof into two main steps.
First, we show that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left(T^{n}f\otimes T^{-n}g-T^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid\mathcal{Z}_{A}]\otimes T^{-n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid\mathcal{Z}_{B}]\right)=0.

(14.2)

Then we prove

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid\mathcal{Z}_{A}](T^{n}x_{1})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid\mathcal{Z}_{B}](T^{-n}x_{2})=(\tilde{f}*\tilde{g})(\pi_{A}(x_{1})+\pi_{B}(x_{2})).

(14.3)

For the first step, we may expand

T^{n}f\otimes T^{-n}g-T^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid\mathcal{Z}_{A}]\otimes T^{-n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid\mathcal{Z}_{B}]\\
=T^{n}\left(f-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid\mathcal{Z}_{A}]\right)\otimes T^{-n}g+T^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid\mathcal{Z}_{A}]\otimes T^{-n}\left(g-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid\mathcal{Z}_{B}]\right),

and it suffices to show that the average of each term converges to 0.
In both cases, one of the functions is orthogonal to the Kronecker factor of the corresponding system.
Thus, it suffices to prove: if
f
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{Z}_{A}
or
g
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{Z}_{B}
, then

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}T^{n}f\otimes T^{-n}g=0.

(14.4)

To this end, we use the van der Corput lemma.
Let
u_{n}=T^{n}f\otimes T^{-n}g
.
Then

\langle u_{n+h},u_{n}\rangle=\int_{X\times X}\left(T^{n+h}f\otimes T^{-n-h}g\right)\left(T^{n}\overline{f}\otimes T^{-n}\overline{g}\right)\penalty\ d(\mu\times\mu).

Applying
(T\times T^{-1})
-invariance of
\mu\times\mu
, we have

\langle u_{n+h},u_{n}\rangle=\left(\int_{X}\overline{f}T^{h}f\penalty\ d\mu\right)\left(\int_{X}\overline{g}T^{-h}g\penalty\ d\mu\right).

Thus,

\limsup_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left|\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\langle u_{n+h},u_{n}\rangle\right|\leqslant\|g\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\limsup_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left|\int_{X}\overline{f}T^{h}f\penalty\ d\mu\right|\leqslant\|g\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\|f\|_{U^{2}}^{2}

and similarly with the roles of
f
and
g
reversed.
Therefore, by the van der Corput lemma, (
14.4
) holds.
This proves (
14.2
).

Now let us prove (
14.3
).
We can realize the Kronecker factors as ergodic group rotations
(Z_{A},m_{Z_{A}},\theta_{A})
and
(Z_{B},m_{Z_{B}},\theta_{B})
, and the factor maps to
(G,m,\theta)
can be obtained as surjective group homomorphisms
\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}:Z_{A}\to G
and
\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}:Z_{B}\to G
such that
\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}(\theta_{A})=\theta
and
\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}(\theta_{B})=\theta
.
Taking
f^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{A}}[f\mid Z_{A}]\in L^{2}(Z_{A})
and
g^{\prime}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{B}}[g\mid Z_{B}]\in L^{2}(Z_{B})
, (
14.3
) reduces to showing that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f^{\prime}(u+n\theta_{A})g^{\prime}(v-n\theta_{B})=(\tilde{f}^{\prime}*\tilde{g}^{\prime})(\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}(u)+\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}(v))

(14.5)

in
L^{2}(m_{A}\times m_{B})
.
Both sides of (
14.5
) are bilinear in the functions
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
.
Since
L^{2}(Z_{A})
and
L^{2}(Z_{B})
are spanned by group characters on
Z_{A}
and
Z_{B}
respectively, it suffices to prove that (
14.5
) holds in the case that
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
are characters.
If
\chi\in\hat{Z}_{A}
, then by orthogonality of characters,

\tilde{\chi}=\begin{cases}\lambda,&\text{if}\penalty\ \chi=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G},\lambda\in\hat{G},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

A similar argument applies to characters on
Z_{B}
.
Now, for the convolution of characters on
G
, the orthogonality relations give for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\hat{G}
,

(\lambda_{1}*\lambda_{2})(x)=\begin{cases}\lambda(x),&\text{if}\penalty\ \lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda,\\
0,&\text{if}\penalty\ \lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}.\end{cases}

Thus, it suffices to show that for
\chi_{A}\in\hat{Z}_{A}
and
\chi_{B}\in\hat{Z}_{B}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\chi_{A}(n\theta_{A})\chi_{B}(-n\theta_{B})=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if}\penalty\ \chi_{A}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \chi_{B}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}\penalty\ \text{for some}\penalty\ \lambda\in\hat{G},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We may write

\chi_{A}(n\theta_{A})\chi_{B}(-n\theta_{B})=\left(\frac{\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})}{\chi_{B}(\theta_{B})}\right)^{n},

so we want to show
\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})=\chi_{B}(\theta_{B})
if and only if there exists
\lambda\in\hat{G}
such that
\chi_{A}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}
and
\chi_{B}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}
.
If
\lambda\in\hat{G}
and
\chi_{A}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}
and
\chi_{B}=\lambda\circ\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}
, then

\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})=\lambda(\theta)=\chi_{B}(\theta_{B}).

Conversely, if
\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})=\chi_{B}(\theta_{B})
, then
\chi_{A}
and
\chi_{B}
are eigenfunctions of
(Z_{A},m_{Z_{A}},\theta_{A})
and
(Z_{B},m_{Z_{B}},\theta_{B})
respectively with the same eigenvalue
\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})=\chi_{B}(\theta_{B})
.
Since
(G,m,\theta)
is maximal among the common rotational factors of these two systems, we can find eigenfunctions
\psi_{A},\psi_{B}
of
(G,m,\theta)
with eigenvalue
\chi_{A}(\theta_{A})=\chi_{B}(\theta_{A})
such that
\chi_{A}=\psi_{A}\circ\pi^{Z_{A}}_{G}
and
\chi_{B}=\psi_{B}\circ\pi^{Z_{B}}_{G}
.
The eigenfunctions of
(G,m,\theta)
are constant multiples of characters, and ergodicity of the system implies that each eigenspace is one-dimensional, so we can write
\psi_{A}=c_{A}\lambda
and
\psi_{B}=c_{B}\lambda
for some
\lambda\in\hat{G}
and constants
c_{A},c_{B}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Finally,
c_{A}=c_{A}\lambda(0)=\psi_{A}(0)=\chi_{A}(0)=1
and similarly
c_{B}=1
.
∎

Proposition 14.8
.

There exist Følner sequences
\Phi,\Psi
such that for any
f\in C(X_{A})
and
g\in C(X_{B})
, we have the following properties:

•

for almost every
x\in X_{B}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Phi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Phi_{N}}f(T_{A}^{n}x_{A})g(T_{B}^{-n}x)=(\tilde{f}*\tilde{g})(\pi_{B}(x))

•

for almost every
x\in X_{A}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Psi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Psi_{N}}f(T_{A}^{-n}x)g(T_{B}^{n}x_{B})=(\tilde{f}*\tilde{g})(\pi_{A}(x)).

Proof.

We will construct the Følner sequence
\Phi
.
The construction of
\Psi
is analogous.

We follow the argument in
[
24
, Lemma 3.12]
.
By Lemma
14.7
and Fubini’s theorem, let
x_{0}\in X_{A}
such that for almost every
x\in X_{B}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(T_{A}^{n}x_{0})g(T_{B}^{-n}x)=(\tilde{f}*\tilde{g})(\pi_{A}(x_{0})+\pi_{B}(x))

for
f\in C(X_{A})
and
g\in C(X_{B})
.

Let
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be countable dense subsets of
C(X_{A})
and
C(X_{B})
respectively.
For
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, let

F_{i,j}(x_{1},x_{2})=(\tilde{f}_{i}*\tilde{g}_{j})(\pi_{A}(x_{1})+\pi_{B}(x_{2})).

Then
F_{i,j}\in C(X_{A}\times X_{B})
is
(T_{A}\times T_{B}^{-1})
-invariant.

The point
x_{A}\in X_{A}
is transitive, so there exists a sequence
(k_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
with
T_{A}^{k_{m}}x_{A}\to x_{0}
.
By refining the sequence
(k_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
so that the convergence occurs very quickly, we can ensure that

\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\|g_{j}\|\cdot\|f_{i}(x_{0})-f_{i}(T_{A}^{k_{m}}x_{A})\|\leqslant 2^{-m}

and

\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\sup_{x_{2}\in X_{B}}\|F_{i,j}(x_{0},x_{2})-F_{i,j}(T_{A}^{k_{m}}x_{A},x_{2})\|\leqslant 2^{-m}.

By the choice of the point
x_{0}
, there exists
N_{m}
such that

H_{m}(x_{2})=\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\left|\frac{1}{N_{m}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{m}}f_{i}(T_{A}^{n}x_{0})g_{j}(T_{B}^{-n}x_{2})-F_{i,j}(x_{0},x_{2})\right|

satisfies
\|H_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}\leqslant 2^{-m}
.
By the triangle inequality, we then have that

\tilde{H}_{m}(x_{2})=\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\left|\frac{1}{N_{m}}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{m}}f_{i}(T_{A}^{n+k_{m}}x_{A})g_{j}(T_{B}^{-n}x_{2})-F_{i,j}(T_{A}^{k_{m}}x_{A},x_{2})\right|

satisfies
\|\tilde{H}_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}\leqslant 3\cdot 2^{-m}
.

Let
\Phi_{m}=\{k_{m}+1,\ldots,k_{m}+N_{m}\}
.
Then since
F_{i,j}
is
(T_{A}\times T_{B}^{-1})
-invariant, we have

\displaystyle H^{\prime}_{m}(x_{2})

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}_{m}(T_{B}^{-k_{m}}x_{2})

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\left|\frac{1}{|\Phi_{m}|}\sum_{n\in\Phi_{m}}f_{i}(T_{A}^{n}x_{A})g_{j}(T_{B}^{-n}x_{2})-F_{i,j}(T_{A}^{k_{m}}x_{A},T_{B}^{-k_{m}}x_{2})\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant m}\left|\frac{1}{|\Phi_{m}|}\sum_{n\in\Phi_{m}}f_{i}(T_{A}^{n}x_{A})g_{j}(T_{B}^{-n}x_{2})-F_{i,j}(x_{A},x_{2})\right|.

Moreover, since
\mu_{B}
is
T_{B}
-invariant, we have
\|H^{\prime}_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}=\|\tilde{H}_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}\leqslant 3\cdot 2^{-m}
.

Let
H=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}H^{\prime}_{m}
.
Then
\|H\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}\leqslant\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\|H^{\prime}_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{B})}<\infty
, so the set
X_{0}=\{x\in X_{B}:H(x)<\infty\}
has full measure.
Suppose
x\in X_{0}
.
Then
H^{\prime}_{m}(x)\to 0
as
m\to\infty
, so

\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Phi_{m}|}\sum_{n\in\Phi_{m}}f_{i}(T_{A}^{n}x_{A})g_{j}(T_{B}^{-n}x)=F_{i,j}(x_{A},x)

for every
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
.
By density of the families
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(g_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
, this completes the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Proposition
14.5
.

Proof of
14.5
.

Property
(1)
is immediate by basic properties of conditional expectation.

(2)
We will prove
E_{A}\subseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}\left(\{\varphi_{A}>0\}\right)
.
The corresponding statement for
B
follows by the same argument.
Let
F=\{\varphi_{A}=0\}\subseteq G
.
Then by the definition of the conditional expectation,

\mu_{A}(E_{A}\cap\pi_{A}^{-1}(F))=\int_{\pi_{A}^{-1}(F)}1_{E_{A}}\penalty\ d\mu_{A}=\int_{F}\varphi_{A}\penalty\ dm=0.

But
\pi_{A}^{-1}(\{\varphi_{A}>0\})=X\backslash\pi_{A}^{-1}(F)
, so we conclude that
E_{A}\subseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(\{\varphi_{A}>0\})
.

(3)
We prove
B+E_{A}\supseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(S)
.
The other part of
(3)
holds by swapping the roles of
A
and
B
.

Let
\Psi
be the Følner sequence given by Proposition
14.8
.
Then for almost every
x\in X_{A}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Psi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Psi_{N}}1_{B}(n)1_{E_{A}}(T_{A}^{-n}x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Psi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Psi_{N}}1_{E_{B}}(T_{B}^{n}x_{B})1_{E_{A}}(T_{A}^{-n}x)

\displaystyle=(\varphi_{A}*\varphi_{B})(\pi_{A}(x)).

Thus,

\displaystyle\pi_{A}^{-1}(S)

\displaystyle=\{x\in X_{A}:(\varphi_{A}*\varphi_{B})(\pi_{A}(x))>0\}

\displaystyle=_{\mu_{A}}\left\{x\in X_{A}:\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Psi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Psi_{N}}1_{B}(n)1_{E_{A}}(T_{A}^{-n}x)>0\right\}.

We want to show that the set on the right hand side is contained in
B+E_{A}
.
Suppose
x\in X_{A}
and

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\Psi_{N}|}\sum_{n\in\Psi_{N}}1_{B}(n)1_{E_{A}}(T_{A}^{-n}x)>0.

Then there exist many values of
n\in B
such that
T_{A}^{-n}x\in E_{A}
.
Hence,
x\in\bigcup_{t\in B}T^{t}E_{A}=B+E_{A}
.

(4)
This is a consequence of
(3)
combined with
14.2
:

m(S)=\mu_{A}(\pi_{A}^{-1}(S))\leqslant\mu_{A}(B+E_{A})\leqslant\alpha+\beta.

∎

14.3.  Finishing the proof

We now want to take the set
S
defined by convolution of functions and replace it with a sumset of measurable subsets of
G
.
This process is enabled by the following lemma.

Lemma 14.9

(
[
13
, Lemma 3.15]
)
.

Let
f,g:G\to[0,1]
be measurable functions.
Let
C=\{f>0\}
,
D=\{g>0\}
, and
E=\{f*g>0\}
.
Then there exist sets
C^{\prime}
and
D^{\prime}
such that
C^{\prime}=_{m}C
,
D^{\prime}=_{m}D
, and the sumset
C^{\prime}+D^{\prime}
is a measurable subset of
E
.

We apply
14.9
to the set
S
from
14.5
to obtain measurable sets
C_{0},D_{0}\subseteq G
such that
C_{0}=_{m}\{\varphi_{A}>0\}
,
D_{0}=_{m}\{\varphi_{B}>0\}
, the sumet
C_{0}+D_{0}
is measurable, and
C_{0}+D_{0}\subseteq S
.
The sets
C_{0}
and
D_{0}
have the following properties:

•

m(C_{0})\geqslant\int_{G}\varphi_{A}\penalty\ dm=\alpha
and
m(D_{0})\geqslant\int_{G}\varphi_{B}\penalty\ dm=\beta
.

•

m(C_{0}+D_{0})\leqslant m(S)\leqslant\alpha+\beta
.

•

E_{A}\subseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0})
and
E_{B}\subseteq_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(D_{0})
.

•

B+E_{A}\supseteq_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
and
A+E_{B}\supseteq_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
.

Combining the inequalities for the measures of
C_{0}
,
D_{0}
, and
C_{0}+D_{0}
, we have
m(C_{0}+D_{0})\leqslant\alpha+\beta\leqslant m(C_{0})+m(D_{0})
.
If we have a strict inequality
m(C_{0}+D_{0})<m(C_{0})+m(D_{0})
, then by Kneser’s theorem for compact abelian groups (
1.1
), the sumset
C_{0}+D_{0}
is contained in a finite union of cosets of a compact open subgroup
H<G
and
m(C_{0}+D_{0})=m(C_{0}+H)+m(D_{0}+H)-m(H)
.
The cosets of
H
partition
G
into disjoint open sets, so we must have
[G:H]\leqslant C_{G}<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
.
The set
D_{0}
satisfies
m(D_{0})\geqslant\beta>1-\frac{1}{[G:H]}
, so
D_{0}
has non-empty intersection with every coset of
H
, i.e.
D_{0}+H=G
.
Thus,

m(C_{0}+D_{0})=m(C_{0}+H)+m(D_{0}+H)-m(H)\geqslant m(H)+1-m(H)=1.

This contradicts the inequality
m(C_{0}+D_{0})\leqslant\alpha+\beta<1
.
Therefore,
m(C_{0}+D_{0})=m(C_{0})+m(D_{0})
, which forces several inequalities to become equalities.
First, from the string of inequalities

\alpha+\beta\leqslant m(C_{0})+m(D_{0})=m(C_{0}+D_{0})\leqslant\alpha+\beta,

we conclude
m(C_{0})=\alpha
,
m(D_{0})=\beta
, and
m(C_{0}+D_{0})=\alpha+\beta
.
We can then deduce

\mu_{A}(\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0})\backslash E_{A})=m(C_{0})-\mu_{A}(E_{A})=\alpha-\alpha=0,

and similarly,
\mu_{B}(\pi_{B}^{-1}(D_{0})\backslash E_{B})=0
, so we in fact have almost equalities of sets
E_{A}=_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0})
and
E_{B}=_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(D_{0})
.
Moreover,

\mu_{B}\left((A+E_{B})\backslash\pi_{B}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})\right)=\mu_{B}(A+E_{B})-m(C_{0}+D_{0})\leqslant\alpha+\beta-(\alpha+\beta)=0.

Similarly,
\mu_{A}\left((B+E_{A})\backslash\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})\right)=0
, so
A+E_{B}=_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
and
B+E_{A}=_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
.

To summarize, we have shown:

•

m(C_{0})=\alpha
and
m(D_{0})=\beta
.

•

m(C_{0}+D_{0})=\alpha+\beta
.

•

E_{A}=_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0})
and
E_{B}=_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(D_{0})
.

•

A+E_{B}=_{\mu_{B}}\pi_{B}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
and
B+E_{A}=_{\mu_{A}}\pi_{A}^{-1}(C_{0}+D_{0})
.

Let
C=C_{0}\cup(A\theta)
and
D=D_{0}\cup(B\theta)
.
Since
A
and
B
are countable sets, we have
C_{0}=_{m}C
and
D_{0}=_{m}D
.
Moreover,

C+D=C_{0}+D_{0}\cup(A\theta+D_{0})\cup(C_{0}+B\theta)\cup(A\theta+B\theta)\\
=_{m}(C_{0}+D_{0})\cup(\underbrace{A+D_{0}}_{=_{m}C_{0}+D_{0}})\cup(\underbrace{B+C_{0}}_{=_{m}C_{0}+D_{0}})=_{m}C_{0}+D_{0}.

So
C
and
D
satisfy:

•

m(C)=\alpha
and
m(D)=\beta
.

•

m(C+D)=m(C)+m(D)
.

•

A\subseteq\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in C\}
and
B\subseteq\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in D\}
.

Now we apply
1.2
to obtain, for some finite index subgroup
H\leqslant G
, a decomposition

C=C^{\prime}+x\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptD=(D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime})+y

with
C^{\prime},D^{\prime}\subseteq H
,
x,y\in G
,
D^{\prime\prime}\subseteq G\backslash H
with
m((G\backslash H)\backslash D^{\prime\prime})=0
such that there exists a surjective homomorphism
\phi:H\to\mathbb{T}
and closed intervals
I,J\subseteq\mathbb{T}
such that

C^{\prime}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(I),\hskip 10.00002ptD^{\prime}\subseteq\phi^{-1}(J),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptm(\phi^{-1}(I)\backslash C^{\prime})=m(\phi^{-1}(J)\backslash D^{\prime})=0.

Let
k=[G:H]\leq C_{G}<\frac{1}{1-\beta}
.
Since
(G,m,\theta)
is ergodic, there exists
a_{0},b_{0}\in\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\}
such that
a_{0}\theta+x\equiv b_{0}\theta+y\equiv 0\pmod{H}
.
Let
\tilde{\theta}\in\mathbb{T}
such that
k\tilde{\theta}=\phi(k\theta)
.
We may then write
A=A_{0}-a_{0}
with

A_{0}=A+a_{0}\subseteq\{n\in\mathbb{N}:(n-a_{0})\theta\in C\}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:(n-a_{0})\theta\in C^{\prime}+x\}\\
\subseteq\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in\underbrace{C^{\prime}+x+a_{0}\theta}_{\subseteq H}\}\subseteq\{n\in k\mathbb{N}:n\tilde{\theta}\in I+\phi(x)+a_{0}\tilde{\theta}\}.

Now,

B+b_{0}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:(n-b_{0})\theta\in D\}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in(D^{\prime}\cup D^{\prime\prime})+\underbrace{(y+b_{0})}_{\in H}\theta\},

so we may write

B_{0}=(B+b_{0})\cap\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in D^{\prime}+(y+b_{0})\theta\}

and

B_{1}=(B+b_{0})\cap\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\in D^{\prime\prime}+(y+b_{0})\theta\}

to get a decomposition
B=(B_{0}\cup B_{1})-b_{0}
with

B_{0}\subseteq\{n\in k\mathbb{N}:n\tilde{\theta}\in J+\phi(y)+b_{0}\tilde{\theta}\}

(14.6)

and

B_{1}\subseteq\{n\in\mathbb{N}:n\theta\notin H\}=\mathbb{N}\backslash k\mathbb{N}.

(14.7)

Let
\tilde{I}=I+\phi(x)+a_{0}\tilde{\theta}
and
\tilde{J}=J+\phi(y)+b_{0}\tilde{\theta}
.
Then
|\tilde{I}|=|I|=m(C^{\prime})=m(C)=\alpha
and
|\tilde{J}|=|J|=m(D^{\prime})=m(D)-m(G\backslash H)=\beta-\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)
.
Let
\tilde{\phi}:k\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{T}
be the map
\tilde{\phi}(n)=n\tilde{\theta}=\phi(n\theta)
.
Then

d(\tilde{\phi}^{-1}(\tilde{I})\backslash A_{0})=|\tilde{I}|-d(A)=\alpha-\alpha=0.

From (
14.6
) and (
14.7
), the sets
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
have upper density bounded by

\overline{d}(B_{0})\leqslant|\tilde{J}|=\beta-\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)

and

\overline{d}(B_{1})\leqslant 1-\frac{1}{k}.

Taking complements, we then have

\underline{d}(B_{0})=\underline{d}((B+b_{0})\backslash B_{1})=\beta-\overline{d}(B_{1})\geqslant\beta-\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)

and

\underline{d}(B_{1})=\underline{d}((B+b_{0})\backslash B_{0})=\beta-\overline{d}(B_{0})\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{k}.

Thus,
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
have density, and

d(\tilde{\phi}^{-1}(\tilde{J})\backslash B_{0})=|\tilde{J}|-d(B_{0})=\beta-\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)-\left(\beta-\left(1-\frac{1}{k}\right)\right)=0

and

d((\mathbb{N}\backslash k\mathbb{N})\backslash B_{1})=1-\frac{1}{k}-d(B_{1})=0.

This proves
14.1
.

15.  Relevant examples

In this section, we present instructive examples to help clarify features of our main result that may not be immediately clear otherwise.

15.1.  Examples of sets satisfying
d(A+B)=d(A)

In light of case
(2)
of
1.4
, it is natural to wonder what type of sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
have the property that
d(A)>0
and
d(A+B)=d(A)
. The following proposition guarantees the existence of such pairs even under the additional restriction that
B
meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 15.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
. There exist a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
d(A)=\alpha
and a set
B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
that meets every residue class in
\mathbb{N}
such that
d(A+B)=d(A)
.

Proof.

Given
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
M<N\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the set

C(t,M,N)=(t\mathbb{Z}+\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\alpha t\rfloor\})\cap[M,N).

It is straightforward to check that

\displaystyle\big|C(t,M,N)\big|

\displaystyle=(N-M)\frac{\lfloor\alpha t\rfloor}{t}+{\rm O}(t),

(15.1)

\displaystyle\big|(C(t,M,N)+\{1,\ldots,b\})\triangle C(t,M,N)\big|

\displaystyle={\rm O}\bigg(\frac{bN}{t}\bigg),\hskip 20.00003pt\forall b,t\in\mathbb{N}.

(15.2)

Let
(b_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(t_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, and
(K_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be any sequences of positive integers such that

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{t_{i}}{K_{i}}=0,

(15.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{i^{2}K_{i}}{b_{i}}=0,

(15.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{b_{i}}{t_{i+1}}=0,

(15.5)

\displaystyle b_{i}\penalty\ \text{is a multiple of}\penalty\ t_{i}.

(15.6)

We claim that if

A=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptB=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(b_{i}+[b_{i-2}])

then
d(A)=\alpha
and
d(A+B)=d(A)
.

From (
15.1
) and (
15.3
) it is straightforward to conclude that the density of
A
exists and equals
\alpha
. Also, from (
15.3
), (
15.4
), and (
15.5
) we see that
d(B)=0
. Since
B
contains arbitrarily long intervals, we also conclude that
B
meets every residue class.

It remains to show that the density of
A+B
exists and equals
\alpha
.
To verify this claim, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be arbitrary, and let
i\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
N\in[K_{i},K_{i+1})
.
In this case,

\displaystyle\big|\big((A+B)\cap[N]\big)

\displaystyle\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|

(15.7)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{s\leqslant i}\big|\big(\big(C(t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})\cap[N])\big|.

Note that
b_{i+1}>N
. So we have

\displaystyle\big|\big(\big(C(t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(

\displaystyle t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})\cap[N])\big|

(15.8)

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\big(C(t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t\leqslant i}\big|\big(C(t_{s},K_{s},K_{s+1})+b_{t}+[b_{t-2}]\big)\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{t\leqslant i}(K_{s+1}+b_{t+2}-K_{s})

\displaystyle={\rm O}(iK_{s+1}).

Since
b_{i}\in[K_{i},K_{i+1})
, we either have
N\leqslant b_{i}
or
N>b_{i}
. Let us first deal with the case
N>b_{i}
. In this case, we get from (
15.7
) and (
15.8
) that

\displaystyle\big|\big(

\displaystyle(A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})\cap[N])\big|+{\rm O}(i^{2}K_{i}).

Since
B\cap[N]\subseteq\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i}\}+[b_{i-2}]
and
C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})\cap[N]=C(t_{i},K_{i},N)
we get

\displaystyle\big|\big(

\displaystyle(A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i}\}+[b_{i-2}]\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},N))\big|+{\rm O}(i^{2}K_{i}).

Since
b_{i}
is a multiple of
t_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i}\}

\displaystyle+[b_{i-2}]\big)\cap[N]

\displaystyle=\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-1}\}+[b_{i-2}]\big)\cap[N]

\displaystyle\subseteq\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+[b_{i-1}]\big)\cap[N].

We obtain

\displaystyle\big|\big(

\displaystyle(A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+[b_{i-1}]\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},N))\big|+{\rm O}(i^{2}K_{i})

\displaystyle=\big|\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},N)+[b_{i-1}]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},N))\big|+{\rm O}(i^{2}K_{i}+b_{i-1})

\displaystyle={\rm O}\bigg(\frac{b_{i-1}N}{t_{i}}+i^{2}K_{i}+b_{i-1}\bigg).

Using (
15.4
) and (
15.5
) and
N>b_{i}
, we see that

{\rm O}\bigg(\frac{b_{i-1}N}{t_{i}}+i^{2}K_{i}+b_{i-1}\bigg)={\rm o}(N)

and hence

\big|\big((A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|={\rm o}(N).

It remains to deal with the case
N\leqslant b_{i}
. In this case, we get from (
15.7
) and (
15.8
) that

\displaystyle\big|\big(

\displaystyle(A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})\cap[N])\big|+{\rm O}(i^{2}K_{i-1}).

The second term in the sum can be estimated as in the previous case to get that

\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i},K_{i},K_{i+1})\cap[N])\big|={\rm O}\bigg(\frac{b_{i-1}N}{t_{i}}+b_{i-1}\bigg).

For the first term we use
b_{i}\geqslant N
and have

\displaystyle\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i-1},

\displaystyle K_{i-1},K_{i})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-1}\}+[b_{i-3}]\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\big(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-1}\}+[b_{i-3}]\big)\backslash C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\big|+{\rm O}(b_{i-1}).

Using that
b_{i-1}
is a multiple of
t_{i-1}
we get

\displaystyle\big|C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+b_{i-1}\backslash C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\big|={\rm O}(b_{i-1})

and hence

\displaystyle\big|\big(\big(C(t_{i-1},

\displaystyle K_{i-1},K_{i})+B\big)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\cap[N])\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\big(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-2}\}+[b_{i-3}]\big)\backslash C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\big|+{\rm O}(b_{i-1}).

Now we can again argue as before to find that

\big|\big(C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})+\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-2}\}+[b_{i-3}]\big)\backslash C(t_{i-1},K_{i-1},K_{i})\big|={\rm O}\bigg(\frac{b_{i-2}N}{t_{i-1}}+b_{i-2}\bigg).

Overall, this implies that

\displaystyle\big|\big((A+B)\cap[N]\big)\backslash(A\cap[N])\big|={\rm O}\bigg(\frac{b_{i-1}N}{t_{i}}+\frac{b_{i-2}N}{t_{i-1}}+b_{i-2}+b_{i-1}+i^{2}K_{i-1}\bigg)={\rm o}(N).

This finishes the proof.
∎

15.2.  Examples of sets satisfying
\underline{d}(A+B)=d(A)+d(B)
and
\overline{d}(A+B)>d(A)+d(B)

Note that it follows from
1.3
that if
\underline{d}(A+B)<d(A)+d(B)
then the density
d(A+B)
exists. A natural question arising from case
(2)
of
1.4
is whether it can happen that

\underline{d}(A+B)=d(A)+d(B)
while
\overline{d}(A+B)>d(A)+d(B)
.
The following proposition implies that this phenomenon can indeed occur.

Proposition 15.2
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{N}=(N_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathbf{M}=(M_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
are sequences in
\mathbb{N}
with
\lim_{s\to\infty}N_{s-1}/M_{s}=\lim_{s\to\infty}M_{s}/N_{s}=0
. Assume
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
with
\alpha<\frac{1}{h}
and
\beta=1-\frac{1}{h}
for some
h\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
A^{*}\subseteq h\mathbb{N}
and
B^{*}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be sets with
d(A^{*})=\alpha
and
d(B^{*})=\beta
.
Then there exist sets
A\subseteq h\mathbb{N}
and
B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with:

(i)

d(A)=\alpha
and
d(B)=\beta
;

(ii)

(A+h)\sim_{\mathbf{N}}A
and
B\sim_{\mathbf{N}}(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})
;

(iii)

\underline{d}(A+B)=d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta
;

(iv)

A\sim_{\mathbf{M}}A^{*}
and
B\sim_{\mathbf{M}}B^{*}
.

Before we provide a proof of
15.2
, let us show how it can be used to deduce the existence of sets with the property that
\underline{d}(A+B)=d(A)+d(B)
and
\overline{d}(A+B)>d(A)+d(B)
.

Let
A^{*}
and
B^{*}
be any sets with

d(A^{*})=\alpha
,
d(B^{*})=\beta
, and

\lim_{K_{2}\to\infty}\lim_{K_{1}\to\infty}\frac{(A^{*}\cap[K_{1}])+(B^{*}\cap[K_{2}])}{K_{1}+K_{2}}=1.

Such sets are easy to construct. For example, one can take
A^{*}
to be a random subset of
\mathbb{N}
obtained by including
n\in\mathbb{N}
in
A^{*}
independently with probability
\alpha
, and
B^{*}
to be the set constructed analogously with probability
\beta
instead of
\alpha
. Using
15.2
, we can then find sets
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{N}
satisfying properties
(i)
–
(iv)
. It follows that

\underline{d}(A+B)=d_{\mathbf{N}}(A+B)=\alpha+\beta\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\overline{d}(A+B)=d_{\mathbf{M}}(A+B)=d_{\mathbf{M}}(A^{*}+B^{*})=1.

Proof of
15.2
.

Let
(K_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(t_{s})_{s\in\mathbb{N}}
be any sequences such that

\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{M_{s}}{t_{s}}=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{t_{s}}{K_{s}}=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{K_{s}}{N_{s}}=0.

Let

\displaystyle A_{s}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=A^{*}\cap(N_{s-1},K_{s}],

\displaystyle A_{s}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(t_{s}\mathbb{N}+\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\alpha ht_{s}\rfloor\})\cap(K_{s},N_{s}]\cap h\mathbb{N},

and

\displaystyle B_{s}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=B^{*}\cap(N_{s-1},M_{s}],

\displaystyle B_{s}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=(N\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cap(M_{s},N_{s}],

Taking

A=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{N}}A_{s}^{\prime}\cup A_{s}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003ptB=\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{N}}B_{s}^{\prime}\cup B_{s}^{\prime\prime}

it is straightforward to check that
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iv)
are satisfied.

It remains to verify
(iii)
.
We have

\displaystyle\big|(A+B)\cap[1,N_{s}]\big|

\displaystyle=\big|(A+B)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\big|+{\rm O}(K_{s})

\displaystyle=\Big|\Big(\big((A\backslash A_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cup A_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big)+\big((B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cup B_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big)\Big)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\Big|+{\rm O}(K_{s}).

Note that
(A\backslash A_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cap[1,N_{s}]\subseteq[1,K_{s}]
and
(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cap[1,N_{s}]\subseteq[1,M_{s}]
.
Hence

\big((A\backslash A_{s}^{\prime\prime})+(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\big)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]=\emptyset.

It follows that

\displaystyle\big|(A

\displaystyle+B)\cap[1,N_{s}]\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\Big(\big((A\backslash A_{s}^{\prime\prime})+B_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big)\cup\big(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\big)\cup\big(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+B_{s}^{\prime\prime}\big)\Big)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\Big|+{\rm O}(K_{s})

\displaystyle=\Big|\big((A+B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cup(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime}))\big)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\Big|+{\rm O}(K_{s}).

Since
A\subseteq h\mathbb{N}
and
B_{s}^{\prime\prime}=(N\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cap(M_{s},N_{s}]
, we see that
(A+B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]=(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]
. This yields

\displaystyle\big|(A

\displaystyle+B)\cap[1,N_{s}]\big|=\Big|\big((\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cup(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime}))\big)\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\Big|+{\rm O}(K_{s}).

Finally, observe that since
A_{s}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq h\mathbb{N}
we have

(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cup(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+(B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime}))=(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cup(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+((B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cap h\mathbb{N}))

and

\big|(A_{s}^{\prime\prime}+((B\backslash B_{s}^{\prime\prime})\cap h\mathbb{N}))\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\big|=\big|A_{s}^{\prime\prime}\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\big|+{\rm O}\Bigg(\frac{M_{s}N_{s}}{t_{s}}\Bigg).

In conclusion, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\big|(A+B)\cap[1,N_{s}]\big|}{N_{s}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\big|(\mathbb{N}\backslash h\mathbb{N})\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]\big|}{N_{s}}+\frac{|A_{s}^{\prime\prime}\cap(2K_{s},N_{s}]|}{N_{s}}+{\rm O}\Bigg(\frac{K_{s}}{N_{s}}+\frac{M_{s}}{t_{s}}\Bigg).

\displaystyle=\beta+\alpha+{\rm o}_{s\to\infty}(1).

∎

16.  Further explorations
